\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\label{lem:uniform_continuity}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

hello worlds
  
\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then

    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).

\label{lem:uniform_continuity}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

hello worlds
  
\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then

    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}

  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}

  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

hello worlds
  
\begin{definition}[Antiderivative]
\label{def:antiderivative}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

A function $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is an \emph{antiderivative} of $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ if $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Continuous on a closed interval]
\label{def:continuous_on}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$. We say $f$ is \emph{continuous on $[a,b]$} if $f$ is continuous at every point of $[a,b]$ (with respect to the subspace topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Has derivative at a point]
\label{def:has_deriv_at}

We say $F : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ \emph{has derivative} $f'$ \emph{at} $x$ if
\[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f'.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$
  is integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A continuous function on a compact set is bounded and measurable, hence integrable
  with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

If $f$ is integrable on $[a,b]$ and $|f(x)| \le M$ for all $x \in [a,b]$, then
  \[
    \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le M \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This follows from the monotonicity of the integral:
  $-M \le f(x) \le M$ implies $-M(b-a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mean Value Theorem]
\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

Let $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$.
  Then there exists $c \in (a,b)$ such that
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standard application of Rolle's theorem to the auxiliary function
  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that
  $|x - y| < \delta$ implies $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$ for all $x, y \in [a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the Heine--Cantor theorem: continuous functions on compact metric spaces are
  uniformly continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Interval integral]
\label{def:interval_integral}

For a function $f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, the \emph{interval integral} $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ is defined as $\int_a^b f\,d\mu$ where $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure. By convention, $\int_a^b f = -\int_b^a f$ when $a > b$.

\begin{lemma}[Continuous functions are integrable]
\label{lem:continuous_integrable}

If $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$

\begin{lemma}[Integral bound]
\label{lem:integral_bound}
\uses{def:interval_integral}

\label{lem:mean_value_theorem}
\uses{def:has_deriv_at}

  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = F'(c) \cdot (b - a).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}

  $g(x) = F(x) - F(a) - \frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}(x-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform continuity on compact intervals]
\label{lem:uniform_continuity}
\uses{def:continuous_on}

If $f$ is continuous on the compact interval $[a,b]$, then $f$ is uniformly continuous
  on $[a,b]$: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that

\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zero derivative implies constant]
\label{lem:zero_deriv_constant}
\uses{lem:mean_value_theorem}

Let $G : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$
  with $G'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$. Then $G$ is constant on $[a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By the Mean Value Theorem (Lemma~\ref{lem:mean_value_theorem}), for any $x, y \in [a,b]$
  with $x < y$, there exists $c \in (x,y)$ such that $G(y) - G(x) = G'(c)(y-x) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc1}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:has_deriv_at, lem:continuous_integrable, lem:integral_bound, lem:uniform_continuity}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$. Define $F : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  \[
    F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in (a,b)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in (a,b)$. We wish to show
  \[
    \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x).
  \]
  By additivity of the integral,
  \[
    F(x+h) - F(x) = \int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt,
  \]
  so
  \[
    \frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(t)\,dt.
  \]
  Since $f(x) = \frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} f(x)\,dt$, we get
  \[
    \left|\frac{F(x+h) - F(x)}{h} - f(x)\right|
    = \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|.
  \]
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform_continuity} (or just continuity at $x$), given $\varepsilon > 0$
  there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|t - x| < \delta$ implies $|f(t) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.
  For $0 < |h| < \delta$, every $t$ between $x$ and $x+h$ satisfies $|t - x| \le |h| < \delta$,
  so by Lemma~\ref{lem:integral_bound},
  \[
    \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_x^{x+h} (f(t) - f(x))\,dt\right|
    \le \frac{1}{|h|} \cdot \varepsilon \cdot |h| = \varepsilon.
  \]
  Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, $F'(x) = f(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus]
\label{thm:ftc2}
\uses{def:continuous_on, def:interval_integral, def:antiderivative, thm:ftc1, lem:zero_deriv_constant}

Let $f : [a,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuous on $[a,b]$, and let $F$ be any antiderivative
  of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Then
  \[
    \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.

  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ftc1}, the function $G(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is an antiderivative
  of $f$. Since both $F$ and $G$ are antiderivatives, $(F - G)'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:zero_deriv_constant}, $F - G$ is constant on $[a,b]$: say
  $F(x) - G(x) = C$ for all $x \in [a,b]$. Then
  \[
    F(b) - F(a) = (G(b) + C) - (G(a) + C) = G(b) - G(a)
    = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt - 0 = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}
